People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hello Dr. Smith. It’s... well, I suppose I’m here because I just don’t have the push I used to have. It’s like my battery just ran plum out. I used to be so busy, you know? I was in the church choir for years and years, every Wednesday evening and Sunday morning, singing all the old hymns, and now... well, I just can't seem to make myself go. The choir director even called a few times, but I just couldn't bring myself to pick up the phone. 

And my quilting, too. I have this big basket of fabric scraps, beautiful little calico prints and solids I was cutting up for a star pattern quilt, and it’s just sitting there in the corner of the front room gathering dust. I look at it, and I know I ought to be working on it, but the want-to is just gone. It just drains right out of my arms. 

Same with visiting folks. I used to bake a little something and go sit on the porch with my neighbors, but now... most days, from sunup to sundown, I just sit right there at home in my armchair. I leave the TV on, just on real low, barely loud enough to hear what they're saying on the programs, just to have a little flickering light in the room, I guess. I sit there for hours and hours. And the Lord knows I know people are expecting me, I know they're wondering where I’ve been hiding myself and why I'm not at church or at the quilting circle, but I just can't find the push to walk out the front door anymore. I don't know where my get-up-and-go went, Doctor, but it just packed up and left me sitting there in the quiet.
=====================================

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no... no, sir, I really can't say that I have. I mean, I've been trying to think on it, looking back over the years, but my whole life... well, ever since I was just a little girl, I've always been a busy bee. We didn't have much time for sitting around back then. I had to leave school after the eighth grade, you see, to help my mama with the younger ones and keep the house running, so I've just always been on my feet. Always doing something. 

Whether it was washing clothes, putting up peach preserves in the late summer, or making sure everybody had a hot supper on the table... I was moving. Even when my husband—God rest his soul—when he was working his shifts, I’d be up way before the sun even thought about rising. I’d have the coffee boiling and the biscuits in the oven, just going and going. I mean, sure, a body gets tired. You get tired after a long day of canning or scrubbing floors, but it was a... a good tired, you know what I mean? You’d sleep sound in your bed and wake up ready to do it all over again the next morning. 

I never had this... this heavy, empty feeling where I just don't care to see a single soul. It’s just completely foreign to me, Doctor. I keep looking back, trying to think if maybe when I was in my thirties, or maybe when the kids were teenagers and driving me half out of my mind, if I ever just sat in the armchair all day with the curtains drawn... but no. I was always buzzing around, always had a project or a visit to make or a church supper to help cook for. I've never been a lazy woman, never in all my sixty-seven years. So this... this just isn't me at all. I've never known a time like this before.
=====================================

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... no, not really. I mean, I try, I really do try to keep myself put together, but it’s just so hard now. Getting dressed... well, some days I just stay in my housecoat all day long. It's a nice housecoat, mind you, a pretty blue floral one my daughter got me for Christmas a couple of years back, but still. I used to never let anyone see me without my hair fixed and a proper dress on, even if it was just the mailman coming up the walk. But now, the thought of running a comb through my hair and picking out an outfit... it just feels like trying to climb a mountain. 

And eating... well, cooking used to be my joy. I’d make a big pot of chicken and dumplings, or a meatloaf with mashed potatoes and green beans cooked down with a little bacon grease, just the way my husband liked it. Now, I might just have a piece of toast or a bowl of cornflakes, if I even remember to eat at all. 

It’s gotten to where... well, I’ve had to step back from things. I used to lead the choir, you know, pick out the hymns and organize the practices, and I helped organize all the little projects down at the senior center, too. We were making these beautiful little crocheted blankets for the premature babies at the hospital, such tiny little things, in soft pinks and yellows. But I just couldn't keep up. I’d promise to have patterns ready or call the other ladies, and I just wouldn't do it. I couldn't. I'd sit there looking at the phone, knowing I should call Mary or Linda, and I just couldn't make my hand pick it up. So I had to tell them I couldn't do it anymore. I just couldn't keep my promises, and that's not like me, Doctor, not like me at all. I've always been a woman of my word. 

Now the ladies from the church... bless their hearts, they mean well, they really do. But they’ve started checking up on me. They come by, bring a casserole or a plate of cookies, and they ask how I’m doing, looking at me with these... these worried eyes. They never used to have to check on me. I was the one checking on everybody else! If somebody was sick, I was the first one at their door with a pot of soup. Now they're coming to my door, and I know they're talking about me, wondering what's wrong with me, why I'm not at choir practice, why I'm not down at the center. It just makes me feel so... so useless, I suppose. Just sitting there in my housecoat while they bring me food I don't even have the appetite to eat.

But now, the thought of running a comb through my hair and picking out an outfit... it just feels like trying to climb a mountain. \n\nAnd eating... well, cooking used to be my joy. I’d make a big pot of chicken and dumplings, or a meatloaf with mashed potatoes and green beans cooked down with a little bacon grease, just the way my husband liked it. Now, I might just have a piece of toast or a bowl of cornflakes, if I even remember to eat at all. \n\nIt’s gotten to where... well, I’ve had to step back from things. I used to lead the choir, you know, pick out the hymns and organize the practices, and I helped organize all the little projects down at the senior center, too. We were making these beautiful little crocheted blankets for the premature babies at the hospital, such tiny little things, in soft pinks and yellows. But I just couldn't keep up. I’d promise to have patterns ready or call the other ladies, and I just wouldn't do it. I couldn't. I'd sit there looking at the phone, knowing I should call Mary or Linda, and I just couldn't make my hand pick it up. So I had to tell them I couldn't do it anymore. I just couldn't keep my promises, and that's not like me, Doctor, not like me at all. I've always been a woman of my word. \n\nNow the ladies from the church... bless their hearts, they mean well, they really do. But they’ve started checking up on me. They come by, bring a casserole or a plate of cookies, and they ask how I’m doing, looking at me with these... these worried eyes. They never used to have to check on me. I was the one checking on everybody else! If somebody was sick, I was the first one at their door with a pot of soup. Now they're coming to my door, and I know they're talking about me, wondering what's wrong with me, why I'm not at choir practice, why I'm not down at the center. It just makes me feel so... so useless, I suppose. Just sitting there in my housecoat while they bring me food I don't even have the appetite to eat.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Well... no, I can't rightly say I feel either of those things. I don't feel jittery or restless, like I need to be pacing holes in the rug or anything like that. I don't tap my foot or wring my hands like my sister Martha used to do when she was fretful. I just sit as still as a stone in that big brown recliner. It’s the one my husband picked out from Sears way back when, real soft corduroy, you know the kind that shows the wear on the armrests? But anyway, I’m not bouncing around in it, I just sit there. 

And as for my thoughts slowing down or my body moving slow... well, I wouldn't say my mind is running like molasses, either. I'm still thinking at my regular speed, I suppose. When I have the television on and I'm watching the game shows, my brain is still working fast enough to know a can of cream of mushroom soup doesn't cost four dollars. I can still follow along with the rules and the flashing lights and all that just fine. It’s just that... well, I don't *do* anything with those thoughts. My body feels normal, and my thinking feels regular... it’s just the doing that’s missing. 

So no, Doctor, I wouldn't say I'm slowed down and I'm certainly not restless. I'll just watch the sunlight move across the living room carpet from morning till late afternoon, watching the little dust motes floating in the air, and I'm just... quiet. Just sitting there, perfectly still, not fast and not slow. Just there.
=====================================

It’s like my battery just ran plum out. I used to be so busy, you know? I was in the church choir for years and years, every Wednesday evening and Sunday morning, singing all the old hymns, and now... well, I just can't seem to make myself go. The choir director even called a few times, but I just couldn't bring myself to pick up the phone. \n\nAnd my quilting, too. I have this big basket of fabric scraps, beautiful little calico prints and solids I was cutting up for a star pattern quilt, and it’s just sitting there in the corner of the front room gathering dust. I look at it, and I know I ought to be working on it, but the want-to is just gone. It just drains right out of my arms. \n\nSame with visiting folks. I used to bake a little something and go sit on the porch with my neighbors, but now... most days, from sunup to sundown, I just sit right there at home in my armchair. I leave the TV on, just on real low, barely loud enough to hear what they're saying on the programs, just to have a little flickering light in the room, I guess. I sit there for hours and hours. And the Lord knows I know people are expecting me, I know they're wondering where I’ve been hiding myself and why I'm not at church or at the quilting circle, but I just can't find the push to walk out the front door anymore. I don't know where my get-up-and-go went, Doctor, but it just packed up and left me sitting there in the quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way for a period of time like this in the past, perhaps years ago?\n\nPatient: Well, no... no, sir, I really can't say that I have. I mean, I've been trying to think on it, looking back over the years, but my whole life... well, ever since I was just a little girl, I've always been a busy bee. We didn't have much time for sitting around back then. I had to leave school after the eighth grade, you see, to help my mama with the younger ones and keep the house running, so I've just always been on my feet. Always doing something. \n\nWhether it was washing clothes, putting up peach preserves in the late summer, or making sure everybody had a hot supper on the table... I was moving. Even when my husband—God rest his soul—when he was working his shifts, I’d be up way before the sun even thought about rising. I’d have the coffee boiling and the biscuits in the oven, just going and going. I mean, sure, a body gets tired. You get tired after a long day of canning or scrubbing floors, but it was a... a good tired, you know what I mean? You’d sleep sound in your bed and wake up ready to do it all over again the next morning. \n\nI never had this... this heavy, empty feeling where I just don't care to see a single soul. It’s just completely foreign to me, Doctor. I keep looking back, trying to think if maybe when I was in my thirties, or maybe when the kids were teenagers and driving me half out of my mind, if I ever just sat in the armchair all day with the curtains drawn... but no. I was always buzzing around, always had a project or a visit to make or a church supper to help cook for. I've never been a lazy woman, never in all my sixty-seven years. So this... this just isn't me at all. I've never known a time like this before.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you been able to keep up with your daily routine, like bathing, getting dressed, and making sure you are eating enough?\n\nPatient: Well... no, not really. I mean, I try, I really do try to keep myself put together, but it’s just so hard now. Getting dressed... well, some days I just stay in my housecoat all day long. It's a nice housecoat, mind you, a pretty blue floral one my daughter got me for Christmas a couple of years back, but still. I used to never let anyone see me without my hair fixed and a proper dress on, even if it was just the mailman coming up the walk. But now, the thought of running a comb through my hair and picking out an outfit... it just feels like trying to climb a mountain. \n\nAnd eating... well, cooking used to be my joy. I’d make a big pot of chicken and dumplings, or a meatloaf with mashed potatoes and green beans cooked down with a little bacon grease, just the way my husband liked it. Now, I might just have a piece of toast or a bowl of cornflakes, if I even remember to eat at all. \n\nIt’s gotten to where... well, I’ve had to step back from things. I used to lead the choir, you know, pick out the hymns and organize the practices, and I helped organize all the little projects down at the senior center, too. We were making these beautiful little crocheted blankets for the premature babies at the hospital, such tiny little things, in soft pinks and yellows. But I just couldn't keep up. I’d promise to have patterns ready or call the other ladies, and I just wouldn't do it. I couldn't. I'd sit there looking at the phone, knowing I should call Mary or Linda, and I just couldn't make my hand pick it up. So I had to tell them I couldn't do it anymore. I just couldn't keep my promises, and that's not like me, Doctor, not like me at all. I've always been a woman of my word. \n\nNow the ladies from the church... bless their hearts, they mean well, they really do. But they’ve started checking up on me. They come by, bring a casserole or a plate of cookies, and they ask how I’m doing, looking at me with these... these worried eyes. They never used to have to check on me. I was the one checking on everybody else! If somebody was sick, I was the first one at their door with a pot of soup. Now they're coming to my door, and I know they're talking about me, wondering what's wrong with me, why I'm not at choir practice, why I'm not down at the center. It just makes me feel so... so useless, I suppose. Just sitting there in my housecoat while they bring me food I don't even have the appetite to eat.\n\nDoctor: When you are sitting in your chair for those long hours, do you feel like your thoughts or movements have slowed down significantly, or do you feel restless and unable to keep still?\n\nPatient: Well... no, I can't rightly say I feel either of those things. I just sit as still as a stone in that big brown recliner. It’s the one my husband picked out from Sears way back when, real soft corduroy, you know the kind that shows the wear on the armrests? I had to leave school after the eighth grade, you see, to help my mama with the younger ones and keep the house running, so I've just always been on my feet. Always doing something. \n\nWhether it was washing clothes, putting up peach preserves in the late summer, or making sure everybody had a hot supper on the table... I was moving. Even when my husband—God rest his soul—when he was working his shifts, I’d be up way before the sun even thought about rising. I’d have the coffee boiling and the biscuits in the oven, just going and going. I mean, sure, a body gets tired. You get tired after a long day of canning or scrubbing floors, but it was a... a good tired, you know what I mean? You’d sleep sound in your bed and wake up ready to do it all over again the next morning. \n\nI never had this... this heavy, empty feeling where I just don't care to see a single soul. It’s just completely foreign to me, Doctor. I keep looking back, trying to think if maybe when I was in my thirties, or maybe when the kids were teenagers and driving me half out of my mind, if I ever just sat in the armchair all day with the curtains drawn... but no. I was always buzzing around, always had a project or a visit to make or a church supper to help cook for. I've never been a lazy woman, never in all my sixty-seven years. So this... this just isn't me at all. I've never known a time like this before.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you been able to keep up with your daily routine, like bathing, getting dressed, and making sure you are eating enough?\n\nPatient: Well... no, not really. I mean, I try, I really do try to keep myself put together, but it’s just so hard now. Getting dressed... well, some days I just stay in my housecoat all day long. It's a nice housecoat, mind you, a pretty blue floral one my daughter got me for Christmas a couple of years back, but still. I used to never let anyone see me without my hair fixed and a proper dress on, even if it was just the mailman coming up the walk. But now, the thought of running a comb through my hair and picking out an outfit... it just feels like trying to climb a mountain. \n\nAnd eating... well, cooking used to be my joy. I’d make a big pot of chicken and dumplings, or a meatloaf with mashed potatoes and green beans cooked down with a little bacon grease, just the way my husband liked it. Now, I might just have a piece of toast or a bowl of cornflakes, if I even remember to eat at all. \n\nIt’s gotten to where... well, I’ve had to step back from things. I used to lead the choir, you know, pick out the hymns and organize the practices, and I helped organize all the little projects down at the senior center, too. We were making these beautiful little crocheted blankets for the premature babies at the hospital, such tiny little things, in soft pinks and yellows. But I just couldn't keep up. I’d promise to have patterns ready or call the other ladies, and I just wouldn't do it. I couldn't. I'd sit there looking at the phone, knowing I should call Mary or Linda, and I just couldn't make my hand pick it up. So I had to tell them I couldn't do it anymore. I just couldn't keep my promises, and that's not like me, Doctor, not like me at all. I've always been a woman of my word. \n\nNow the ladies from the church... bless their hearts, they mean well, they really do. But they’ve started checking up on me. They come by, bring a casserole or a plate of cookies, and they ask how I’m doing, looking at me with these... these worried eyes. They never used to have to check on me. I was the one checking on everybody else! If somebody was sick, I was the first one at their door with a pot of soup. Now they're coming to my door, and I know they're talking about me, wondering what's wrong with me, why I'm not at choir practice, why I'm not down at the center. It just makes me feel so... so useless, I suppose. Just sitting there in my housecoat while they bring me food I don't even have the appetite to eat.\n\nDoctor: When you are sitting in your chair for those long hours, do you feel like your thoughts or movements have slowed down significantly, or do you feel restless and unable to keep still?\n\nPatient: Well... no, I can't rightly say I feel either of those things. I don't feel jittery or restless, like I need to be pacing holes in the rug or anything like that. I don't tap my foot or wring my hands like my sister Martha used to do when she was fretful. I just sit as still as a stone in that big brown recliner. It’s the one my husband picked out from Sears way back when, real soft corduroy, you know the kind that shows the wear on the armrests? But anyway, I’m not bouncing around in it, I just sit there. \n\nAnd as for my thoughts slowing down or my body moving slow... well, I wouldn't say my mind is running like molasses, either. I'm still thinking at my regular speed, I suppose. When I have the television on and I'm watching the game shows, my brain is still working fast enough to know a can of cream of mushroom soup doesn't cost four dollars. I can still follow along with the rules and the flashing lights and all that just fine. It’s just that... well, I don't *do* anything with those thoughts. My body feels normal, and my thinking feels regular... it’s just the doing that’s missing. \n\nSo no, Doctor, I wouldn't say I'm slowed down and I'm certainly not restless. I'll just watch the sunlight move across the living room carpet from morning till late afternoon, watching the little dust motes floating in the air, and I'm just... quiet. Just sitting there, perfectly still, not fast and not slow. Just there.\n\n[Last Question]\nYou mentioned feeling useless because the ladies from church are checking on you; do you find yourself feeling guilty or like you are a burden to others because of these changes in your life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Guilty? Well, no, I wouldn't use that word. I don't feel guilty, Doctor, and I certainly don't feel like a burden. I mean, if you really stop and think about it, I’ve spent my entire life taking care of other folks. Like I was saying before, ever since I had to leave school to help Mama, I have been the one doing the heavy lifting. I’ve baked more casseroles, fried more chicken, and dropped off more Tupperware containers on people's front porches than I could ever begin to count. 

So when Betty or Sarah—Sarah’s the one from the choir who makes that wonderful macaroni and cheese, you know, the kind where you bake it in the oven with the crushed-up Ritz crackers and a little butter on top so it gets real golden brown and crispy—when they come over, I don't feel like I'm putting them out. I know they don't mind doing it. It’s just what we’ve all always done for each other in our congregation. It’s the Christian thing to do, visiting folks when they're down, even if I’m not exactly sick with a fever or laid up with a broken hip. 

I don't feel bad that they’re spending their afternoon driving over, or spending their money on a pound of hamburger meat to make me a supper. I've earned a little bit of looking after, I suppose, after all these years of being the one running the show and organizing the meal trains. So no, they aren't burdened by me. It’s just that... well, I just don't like being on the receiving end of it. It just makes me feel silly. Like a bump on a log. 

It’s that feeling of being useless, just like I said. I sit there in my recliner while they set a hot plate on my coffee table, and I just wish I was the one in the kitchen. I wish I was the one doing the providing. But guilty? No, sir. I don't sit around wringing my hands feeling guilty after they leave. I just watch them drive away out the front window and feel... well, just empty, I guess. But definitely not a burden. I just wish I had my old self back so I could return the favor, but I don't feel guilty about it.
=====================================

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... no, I can't say my sleep has changed much at all. I've always been a real good sleeper, praise the Lord. Even with everything else feeling so... so out of sorts lately, my nights are just about the exact same as they’ve always been. I still go to bed right after the 10 o'clock news. I always watch Channel 5—they have that new young weatherman now, the one who talks just a little too fast, but he seems like a nice enough boy. Anyway, as soon as he gives the extended forecast, I turn off the set, make sure the deadbolt on the front door is turned, check the lock on the back screen door, and head on back to the bedroom.

I don't toss and turn, and I certainly don't lay there staring up at the ceiling fan counting sheep. I just pull the covers up—I still use that heavy quilt my grandmother made, the one with the double wedding ring pattern, even in the summertime because I just like the weight of it on my legs—and I’m out like a light before my head even really settles into the pillow. 

And I don't sleep the day away, either. No, sir. My eyes pop open right around 6:15 or 6:30 every single morning, regular as a church bell. It's just a habit from all those years of getting up early to fry eggs and make a pot of percolator coffee for my husband before he had to be down at the plant for his shift. You do something for forty years, your body just remembers, I suppose. 

So, no, I'm not sleeping more, and I'm not waking up in the wee hours of the morning unable to get back to sleep. I get my solid eight hours just like I always have. It’s just... well, it’s what happens after I wake up that's the problem. I get up, use the washroom, and then I just... migrate right back to that brown recliner and sit. But the sleeping part? No, Doctor, that’s working just fine. I haven't noticed any trouble there at all.

I was in the church choir for years and years, every Wednesday evening and Sunday morning, singing all the old hymns, and now... well, I just can't seem to make myself go. Now they're coming to my door, and I know they're talking about me, wondering what's wrong with me, why I'm not at choir practice, why I'm not down at the center. It just makes me feel so... so useless, I suppose. Just sitting there in my housecoat while they bring me food I don't even have the appetite to eat.\n\nDoctor: When you are sitting in your chair for those long hours, do you feel like your thoughts or movements have slowed down significantly, or do you feel restless and unable to keep still?\n\nPatient: Well... no, I can't rightly say I feel either of those things. I don't feel jittery or restless, like I need to be pacing holes in the rug or anything like that. I don't tap my foot or wring my hands like my sister Martha used to do when she was fretful. I just sit as still as a stone in that big brown recliner. It’s the one my husband picked out from Sears way back when, real soft corduroy, you know the kind that shows the wear on the armrests? But anyway, I’m not bouncing around in it, I just sit there. \n\nAnd as for my thoughts slowing down or my body moving slow... well, I wouldn't say my mind is running like molasses, either. I'm still thinking at my regular speed, I suppose. When I have the television on and I'm watching the game shows, my brain is still working fast enough to know a can of cream of mushroom soup doesn't cost four dollars. I can still follow along with the rules and the flashing lights and all that just fine. It’s just that... well, I don't *do* anything with those thoughts. My body feels normal, and my thinking feels regular... it’s just the doing that’s missing. \n\nSo no, Doctor, I wouldn't say I'm slowed down and I'm certainly not restless. I'll just watch the sunlight move across the living room carpet from morning till late afternoon, watching the little dust motes floating in the air, and I'm just... quiet. Just sitting there, perfectly still, not fast and not slow. Just there.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling useless because the ladies from church are checking on you; do you find yourself feeling guilty or like you are a burden to others because of these changes in your life?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Well, no, I wouldn't use that word. I don't feel guilty, Doctor, and I certainly don't feel like a burden. I mean, if you really stop and think about it, I’ve spent my entire life taking care of other folks. Like I was saying before, ever since I had to leave school to help Mama, I have been the one doing the heavy lifting. I’ve baked more casseroles, fried more chicken, and dropped off more Tupperware containers on people's front porches than I could ever begin to count. \n\nSo when Betty or Sarah—Sarah’s the one from the choir who makes that wonderful macaroni and cheese, you know, the kind where you bake it in the oven with the crushed-up Ritz crackers and a little butter on top so it gets real golden brown and crispy—when they come over, I don't feel like I'm putting them out. I know they don't mind doing it. It’s just what we’ve all always done for each other in our congregation. It’s the Christian thing to do, visiting folks when they're down, even if I’m not exactly sick with a fever or laid up with a broken hip. \n\nI don't feel bad that they’re spending their afternoon driving over, or spending their money on a pound of hamburger meat to make me a supper. I've earned a little bit of looking after, I suppose, after all these years of being the one running the show and organizing the meal trains. So no, they aren't burdened by me. It’s just that... well, I just don't like being on the receiving end of it. It just makes me feel silly. Like a bump on a log. \n\nIt’s that feeling of being useless, just like I said. I sit there in my recliner while they set a hot plate on my coffee table, and I just wish I was the one in the kitchen. I wish I was the one doing the providing. But guilty? No, sir. I don't sit around wringing my hands feeling guilty after they leave. I just watch them drive away out the front window and feel... well, just empty, I guess. But definitely not a burden. I just wish I had my old self back so I could return the favor, but I don't feel guilty about it.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you noticed any changes in your sleep, such as having trouble falling asleep, waking up too early in the morning, or perhaps sleeping much more than you used to?\n\nPatient: Well... no, I can't say my sleep has changed much at all. I've always been a real good sleeper, praise the Lord. Even with everything else feeling so... so out of sorts lately, my nights are just about the exact same as they’ve always been. I still go to bed right after the 10 o'clock news. I always watch Channel 5—they have that new young weatherman now, the one who talks just a little too fast, but he seems like a nice enough boy. Anyway, as soon as he gives the extended forecast, I turn off the set, make sure the deadbolt on the front door is turned, check the lock on the back screen door, and head on back to the bedroom.\n\nI don't toss and turn, and I certainly don't lay there staring up at the ceiling fan counting sheep. I just pull the covers up—I still use that heavy quilt my grandmother made, the one with the double wedding ring pattern, even in the summertime because I just like the weight of it on my legs—and I’m out like a light before my head even really settles into the pillow. \n\nAnd I don't sleep the day away, either. No, sir. My eyes pop open right around 6:15 or 6:30 every single morning, regular as a church bell. It's just a habit from all those years of getting up early to fry eggs and make a pot of percolator coffee for my husband before he had to be down at the plant for his shift. You do something for forty years, your body just remembers, I suppose. \n\nSo, no, I'm not sleeping more, and I'm not waking up in the wee hours of the morning unable to get back to sleep. I get my solid eight hours just like I always have. It’s just... well, it’s what happens after I wake up that's the problem. I get up, use the washroom, and then I just... migrate right back to that brown recliner and sit. But the sleeping part? No, Doctor, that’s working just fine. I haven't noticed any trouble there at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

    "role": "system",

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: When you are sitting in your chair for those long hours, do you feel like your thoughts or movements have slowed down significantly, or do you feel restless and unable to keep still?\n\nPatient: Well... no, I can't rightly say I feel either of those things. I don't feel jittery or restless, like I need to be pacing holes in the rug or anything like that. I don't tap my foot or wring my hands like my sister Martha used to do when she was fretful. I just sit as still as a stone in that big brown recliner. It’s the one my husband picked out from Sears way back when, real soft corduroy, you know the kind that shows the wear on the armrests? But anyway, I’m not bouncing around in it, I just sit there. \n\nAnd as for my thoughts slowing down or my body moving slow... well, I wouldn't say my mind is running like molasses, either. I'm still thinking at my regular speed, I suppose. When I have the television on and I'm watching the game shows, my brain is still working fast enough to know a can of cream of mushroom soup doesn't cost four dollars. I can still follow along with the rules and the flashing lights and all that just fine. It’s just that... well, I don't *do* anything with those thoughts. My body feels normal, and my thinking feels regular... it’s just the doing that’s missing. \n\nSo no, Doctor, I wouldn't say I'm slowed down and I'm certainly not restless. I'll just watch the sunlight move across the living room carpet from morning till late afternoon, watching the little dust motes floating in the air, and I'm just... quiet. Just sitting there, perfectly still, not fast and not slow. Just there.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling useless because the ladies from church are checking on you; do you find yourself feeling guilty or like you are a burden to others because of these changes in your life?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Well, no, I wouldn't use that word. I don't feel guilty, Doctor, and I certainly don't feel like a burden. I mean, if you really stop and think about it, I’ve spent my entire life taking care of other folks. Like I was saying before, ever since I had to leave school to help Mama, I have been the one doing the heavy lifting. I’ve baked more casseroles, fried more chicken, and dropped off more Tupperware containers on people's front porches than I could ever begin to count. \n\nSo when Betty or Sarah—Sarah’s the one from the choir who makes that wonderful macaroni and cheese, you know, the kind where you bake it in the oven with the crushed-up Ritz crackers and a little butter on top so it gets real golden brown and crispy—when they come over, I don't feel like I'm putting them out. I know they don't mind doing it. It’s just what we’ve all always done for each other in our congregation. It’s the Christian thing to do, visiting folks when they're down, even if I’m not exactly sick with a fever or laid up with a broken hip. \n\nI don't feel bad that they’re spending their afternoon driving over, or spending their money on a pound of hamburger meat to make me a supper. I've earned a little bit of looking after, I suppose, after all these years of being the one running the show and organizing the meal trains. So no, they aren't burdened by me. It’s just that... well, I just don't like being on the receiving end of it. It just makes me feel silly. Like a bump on a log. \n\nIt’s that feeling of being useless, just like I said. I sit there in my recliner while they set a hot plate on my coffee table, and I just wish I was the one in the kitchen. I wish I was the one doing the providing. But guilty? No, sir. I don't sit around wringing my hands feeling guilty after they leave. I just watch them drive away out the front window and feel... well, just empty, I guess. But definitely not a burden. I just wish I had my old self back so I could return the favor, but I don't feel guilty about it.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you noticed any changes in your sleep, such as having trouble falling asleep, waking up too early in the morning, or perhaps sleeping much more than you used to?\n\nPatient: Well... no, I can't say my sleep has changed much at all. I've always been a real good sleeper, praise the Lord. Even with everything else feeling so... so out of sorts lately, my nights are just about the exact same as they’ve always been. I still go to bed right after the 10 o'clock news. I always watch Channel 5—they have that new young weatherman now, the one who talks just a little too fast, but he seems like a nice enough boy. Anyway, as soon as he gives the extended forecast, I turn off the set, make sure the deadbolt on the front door is turned, check the lock on the back screen door, and head on back to the bedroom.\n\nI don't toss and turn, and I certainly don't lay there staring up at the ceiling fan counting sheep. I just pull the covers up—I still use that heavy quilt my grandmother made, the one with the double wedding ring pattern, even in the summertime because I just like the weight of it on my legs—and I’m out like a light before my head even really settles into the pillow. \n\nAnd I don't sleep the day away, either. No, sir. My eyes pop open right around 6:15 or 6:30 every single morning, regular as a church bell. It's just a habit from all those years of getting up early to fry eggs and make a pot of percolator coffee for my husband before he had to be down at the plant for his shift. You do something for forty years, your body just remembers, I suppose. \n\nSo, no, I'm not sleeping more, and I'm not waking up in the wee hours of the morning unable to get back to sleep. I get my solid eight hours just like I always have. It’s just... well, it’s what happens after I wake up that's the problem. I get up, use the washroom, and then I just... migrate right back to that brown recliner and sit. But the sleeping part? No, Doctor, that’s working just fine. I haven't noticed any trouble there at all.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you seen a doctor recently for any new medical conditions, or have you started taking any new medications since these changes began?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, I haven't. I don't go running to the doctor for every little ache and pain, Lord knows. The only doctor I see is Dr. Evans down at the family practice, and I haven't been in to see him since... oh, it must have been early April for my regular yearly checkup. He always listens to my heart and has me breathe deep, and he told me I was healthy as a horse for a woman my age. He said everything was looking just fine.

And as for medications, no, nothing new at all. To tell you the truth, I don't take any prescription pills whatsoever. Never have needed to, praise the Lord. I just take a daily multivitamin, the kind you get in the big plastic bottle at the grocery store. I have one of those little pill organizers, you know the ones with the days of the week printed on the lids in big black letters? My daughter bought it for me at the Dollar General because she thought I'd forget to take my vitamin, but I never do. I take it every morning right after I brush my teeth. I usually buy them down at the corner pharmacy—well, it’s a CVS now, but I still call it the Rexall because old Mr. Higgins used to run it before the big company bought him out. He used to give the kids hard candies over the counter, the kind wrapped in that shiny strawberry paper. 

But anyway, no, no new medicines and no new doctor visits. I don't even like taking an aspirin for a headache if I can help it. Mama always said a body shouldn't rely on too many powders and pills if a good cup of tea and a rest could fix it. So no, Doctor, no new medical problems, no new pills, nothing like that. My physical health is just as good as it ever was. It’s just... like I said, I just sit there in that recliner. My body is right as rain, it's just doing a whole lot of nothing.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F06.30, F03.90\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I'm Dr. Smith. It's nice to meet you; could you tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello Dr. Smith. It’s... well, I suppose I’m here because I just don’t have the push I used to have. It’s like my battery just ran plum out. I used to be so busy, you know? I was in the church choir for years and years, every Wednesday evening and Sunday morning, singing all the old hymns, and now... well, I just can't seem to make myself go. The choir director even called a few times, but I just couldn't bring myself to pick up the phone. \n\nAnd my quilting, too. I have this big basket of fabric scraps, beautiful little calico prints and solids I was cutting up for a star pattern quilt, and it’s just sitting there in the corner of the front room gathering dust. I look at it, and I know I ought to be working on it, but the want-to is just gone. It just drains right out of my arms. \n\nSame with visiting folks. I used to bake a little something and go sit on the porch with my neighbors, but now... most days, from sunup to sundown, I just sit right there at home in my armchair. I leave the TV on, just on real low, barely loud enough to hear what they're saying on the programs, just to have a little flickering light in the room, I guess. I sit there for hours and hours. And the Lord knows I know people are expecting me, I know they're wondering where I’ve been hiding myself and why I'm not at church or at the quilting circle, but I just can't find the push to walk out the front door anymore. I don't know where my get-up-and-go went, Doctor, but it just packed up and left me sitting there in the quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way for a period of time like this in the past, perhaps years ago?\n\nPatient: Well, no... no, sir, I really can't say that I have. I mean, I've been trying to think on it, looking back over the years, but my whole life... well, ever since I was just a little girl, I've always been a busy bee. We didn't have much time for sitting around back then. I had to leave school after the eighth grade, you see, to help my mama with the younger ones and keep the house running, so I've just always been on my feet. Always doing something. \n\nWhether it was washing clothes, putting up peach preserves in the late summer, or making sure everybody had a hot supper on the table... I was moving. Even when my husband—God rest his soul—when he was working his shifts, I’d be up way before the sun even thought about rising. I’d have the coffee boiling and the biscuits in the oven, just going and going. I mean, sure, a body gets tired. You get tired after a long day of canning or scrubbing floors, but it was a... a good tired, you know what I mean? You’d sleep sound in your bed and wake up ready to do it all over again the next morning. \n\nI never had this... this heavy, empty feeling where I just don't care to see a single soul. It’s just completely foreign to me, Doctor. I keep looking back, trying to think if maybe when I was in my thirties, or maybe when the kids were teenagers and driving me half out of my mind, if I ever just sat in the armchair all day with the curtains drawn... but no. I was always buzzing around, always had a project or a visit to make or a church supper to help cook for. I've never been a lazy woman, never in all my sixty-seven years. So this... this just isn't me at all. I've never known a time like this before.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you been able to keep up with your daily routine, like bathing, getting dressed, and making sure you are eating enough?\n\nPatient: Well... no, not really. I mean, I try, I really do try to keep myself put together, but it’s just so hard now. Getting dressed... well, some days I just stay in my housecoat all day long. It's a nice housecoat, mind you, a pretty blue floral one my daughter got me for Christmas a couple of years back, but still. I used to never let anyone see me without my hair fixed and a proper dress on, even if it was just the mailman coming up the walk. But now, the thought of running a comb through my hair and picking out an outfit... it just feels like trying to climb a mountain. \n\nAnd eating... well, cooking used to be my joy. I’d make a big pot of chicken and dumplings, or a meatloaf with mashed potatoes and green beans cooked down with a little bacon grease, just the way my husband liked it. Now, I might just have a piece of toast or a bowl of cornflakes, if I even remember to eat at all. \n\nIt’s gotten to where... well, I’ve had to step back from things. I used to lead the choir, you know, pick out the hymns and organize the practices, and I helped organize all the little projects down at the senior center, too. We were making these beautiful little crocheted blankets for the premature babies at the hospital, such tiny little things, in soft pinks and yellows. But I just couldn't keep up. I’d promise to have patterns ready or call the other ladies, and I just wouldn't do it. I couldn't. I'd sit there looking at the phone, knowing I should call Mary or Linda, and I just couldn't make my hand pick it up. So I had to tell them I couldn't do it anymore. I just couldn't keep my promises, and that's not like me, Doctor, not like me at all. I've always been a woman of my word. \n\nNow the ladies from the church... bless their hearts, they mean well, they really do. But they’ve started checking up on me. They come by, bring a casserole or a plate of cookies, and they ask how I’m doing, looking at me with these... these worried eyes. They never used to have to check on me. I was the one checking on everybody else! If somebody was sick, I was the first one at their door with a pot of soup. Now they're coming to my door, and I know they're talking about me, wondering what's wrong with me, why I'm not at choir practice, why I'm not down at the center. It just makes me feel so... so useless, I suppose. Just sitting there in my housecoat while they bring me food I don't even have the appetite to eat.\n\nDoctor: When you are sitting in your chair for those long hours, do you feel like your thoughts or movements have slowed down significantly, or do you feel restless and unable to keep still?\n\nPatient: Well... no, I can't rightly say I feel either of those things. I don't feel jittery or restless, like I need to be pacing holes in the rug or anything like that. I don't tap my foot or wring my hands like my sister Martha used to do when she was fretful. I just sit as still as a stone in that big brown recliner. It’s the one my husband picked out from Sears way back when, real soft corduroy, you know the kind that shows the wear on the armrests? I'll just watch the sunlight move across the living room carpet from morning till late afternoon, watching the little dust motes floating in the air, and I'm just... quiet. Just there.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling useless because the ladies from church are checking on you; do you find yourself feeling guilty or like you are a burden to others because of these changes in your life?\n\nPatient: Guilty? But now, the thought of running a comb through my hair and picking out an outfit... it just feels like trying to climb a mountain. \n\nAnd eating... well, cooking used to be my joy. I’d make a big pot of chicken and dumplings, or a meatloaf with mashed potatoes and green beans cooked down with a little bacon grease, just the way my husband liked it. Now, I might just have a piece of toast or a bowl of cornflakes, if I even remember to eat at all. \n\nIt’s gotten to where... well, I’ve had to step back from things. I used to lead the choir, you know, pick out the hymns and organize the practices, and I helped organize all the little projects down at the senior center, too. We were making these beautiful little crocheted blankets for the premature babies at the hospital, such tiny little things, in soft pinks and yellows. But I just couldn't keep up. I’d promise to have patterns ready or call the other ladies, and I just wouldn't do it. I couldn't. I'd sit there looking at the phone, knowing I should call Mary or Linda, and I just couldn't make my hand pick it up. So I had to tell them I couldn't do it anymore. I just couldn't keep my promises, and that's not like me, Doctor, not like me at all. I've always been a woman of my word. \n\nNow the ladies from the church... bless their hearts, they mean well, they really do. But they’ve started checking up on me. They come by, bring a casserole or a plate of cookies, and they ask how I’m doing, looking at me with these... these worried eyes. They never used to have to check on me. I was the one checking on everybody else! If somebody was sick, I was the first one at their door with a pot of soup. Now they're coming to my door, and I know they're talking about me, wondering what's wrong with me, why I'm not at choir practice, why I'm not down at the center. It just makes me feel so... so useless, I suppose. Just sitting there in my housecoat while they bring me food I don't even have the appetite to eat.\n\nDoctor: When you are sitting in your chair for those long hours, do you feel like your thoughts or movements have slowed down significantly, or do you feel restless and unable to keep still?\n\nPatient: Well... no, I can't rightly say I feel either of those things. I don't feel jittery or restless, like I need to be pacing holes in the rug or anything like that. I don't tap my foot or wring my hands like my sister Martha used to do when she was fretful. I just sit as still as a stone in that big brown recliner. It’s the one my husband picked out from Sears way back when, real soft corduroy, you know the kind that shows the wear on the armrests? But anyway, I’m not bouncing around in it, I just sit there. \n\nAnd as for my thoughts slowing down or my body moving slow... well, I wouldn't say my mind is running like molasses, either. I'm still thinking at my regular speed, I suppose. When I have the television on and I'm watching the game shows, my brain is still working fast enough to know a can of cream of mushroom soup doesn't cost four dollars. I can still follow along with the rules and the flashing lights and all that just fine. It’s just that... well, I don't *do* anything with those thoughts. My body feels normal, and my thinking feels regular... it’s just the doing that’s missing. \n\nSo no, Doctor, I wouldn't say I'm slowed down and I'm certainly not restless. I'll just watch the sunlight move across the living room carpet from morning till late afternoon, watching the little dust motes floating in the air, and I'm just... quiet. Just sitting there, perfectly still, not fast and not slow. Just there.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling useless because the ladies from church are checking on you; do you find yourself feeling guilty or like you are a burden to others because of these changes in your life?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Well, no, I wouldn't use that word. I don't feel guilty, Doctor, and I certainly don't feel like a burden. I mean, if you really stop and think about it, I’ve spent my entire life taking care of other folks. Like I was saying before, ever since I had to leave school to help Mama, I have been the one doing the heavy lifting. I’ve baked more casseroles, fried more chicken, and dropped off more Tupperware containers on people's front porches than I could ever begin to count. \n\nSo when Betty or Sarah—Sarah’s the one from the choir who makes that wonderful macaroni and cheese, you know, the kind where you bake it in the oven with the crushed-up Ritz crackers and a little butter on top so it gets real golden brown and crispy—when they come over, I don't feel like I'm putting them out. I know they don't mind doing it. It’s just what we’ve all always done for each other in our congregation. It’s the Christian thing to do, visiting folks when they're down, even if I’m not exactly sick with a fever or laid up with a broken hip. \n\nI don't feel bad that they’re spending their afternoon driving over, or spending their money on a pound of hamburger meat to make me a supper. I've earned a little bit of looking after, I suppose, after all these years of being the one running the show and organizing the meal trains. So no, they aren't burdened by me. It’s just that... well, I just don't like being on the receiving end of it. It just makes me feel silly. Like a bump on a log. \n\nIt’s that feeling of being useless, just like I said. I sit there in my recliner while they set a hot plate on my coffee table, and I just wish I was the one in the kitchen. I wish I was the one doing the providing. But guilty? No, sir. I don't sit around wringing my hands feeling guilty after they leave. I just watch them drive away out the front window and feel... well, just empty, I guess. But definitely not a burden. I just wish I had my old self back so I could return the favor, but I don't feel guilty about it.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you noticed any changes in your sleep, such as having trouble falling asleep, waking up too early in the morning, or perhaps sleeping much more than you used to?\n\nPatient: Well... no, I can't say my sleep has changed much at all. I've always been a real good sleeper, praise the Lord. Even with everything else feeling so... so out of sorts lately, my nights are just about the exact same as they’ve always been. I still go to bed right after the 10 o'clock news. I always watch Channel 5—they have that new young weatherman now, the one who talks just a little too fast, but he seems like a nice enough boy. Anyway, as soon as he gives the extended forecast, I turn off the set, make sure the deadbolt on the front door is turned, check the lock on the back screen door, and head on back to the bedroom.\n\nI don't toss and turn, and I certainly don't lay there staring up at the ceiling fan counting sheep. I just pull the covers up—I still use that heavy quilt my grandmother made, the one with the double wedding ring pattern, even in the summertime because I just like the weight of it on my legs—and I’m out like a light before my head even really settles into the pillow. \n\nAnd I don't sleep the day away, either. No, sir. My eyes pop open right around 6:15 or 6:30 every single morning, regular as a church bell. It's just a habit from all those years of getting up early to fry eggs and make a pot of percolator coffee for my husband before he had to be down at the plant for his shift. You do something for forty years, your body just remembers, I suppose. \n\nSo, no, I'm not sleeping more, and I'm not waking up in the wee hours of the morning unable to get back to sleep. I get my solid eight hours just like I always have. It’s just... well, it’s what happens after I wake up that's the problem. I get up, use the washroom, and then I just... migrate right back to that brown recliner and sit. But the sleeping part? No, Doctor, that’s working just fine. I haven't noticed any trouble there at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you seen a doctor recently for any new medical conditions, or have you started taking any new medications since these changes began?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I haven't. I don't go running to the doctor for every little ache and pain, Lord knows. The only doctor I see is Dr. Evans down at the family practice, and I haven't been in to see him since... oh, it must have been early April for my regular yearly checkup. He always listens to my heart and has me breathe deep, and he told me I was healthy as a horse for a woman my age. He said everything was looking just fine.\n\nAnd as for medications, no, nothing new at all. To tell you the truth, I don't take any prescription pills whatsoever. Never have needed to, praise the Lord. I just take a daily multivitamin, the kind you get in the big plastic bottle at the grocery store. I have one of those little pill organizers, you know the ones with the days of the week printed on the lids in big black letters? My daughter bought it for me at the Dollar General because she thought I'd forget to take my vitamin, but I never do. I take it every morning right after I brush my teeth. I usually buy them down at the corner pharmacy—well, it’s a CVS now, but I still call it the Rexall because old Mr. Higgins used to run it before the big company bought him out. He used to give the kids hard candies over the counter, the kind wrapped in that shiny strawberry paper. \n\nBut anyway, no, no new medicines and no new doctor visits. I don't even like taking an aspirin for a headache if I can help it. Mama always said a body shouldn't rely on too many powders and pills if a good cup of tea and a rest could fix it. So no, Doctor, no new medical problems, no new pills, nothing like that. My physical health is just as good as it ever was. It’s just... like I said, I just sit there in that recliner. My body is right as rain, it's just doing a whole lot of nothing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh heavens no, Doctor. I really meant it when I said I haven't ever felt like this in all my sixty-seven years on this earth. Never once. You see, I was always the kind of person who couldn't sit still for more than five minutes unless I was shelling peas or snapping green beans on the front porch. Even when I was just a little girl, after I had to leave school in the eighth grade to help Mama around the house—because she had her hands full with the younger ones and Daddy was working down at the lumber yard from sunup to sundown—I was always moving. I was always doing something useful. 

And then when I got married to my husband, Lord rest his soul, there was always a house to keep, clothes to wash, meals to cook. I’d be up with the chickens and wouldn't stop until the streetlights came on. I never had a day where I just... sat. Even when I was tired, it was a good kind of tired, you know? The kind where your bones ache a little but you know you put in a hard day's work and you can be proud of it. I never had this... this heavy, empty feeling where I just don't care to do a single blessed thing. 

So no, nothing like this has ever happened to me before. Not in my twenties, not in my forties, not when the kids grew up and moved out of the house, never. It's completely brand new, and I just don't understand it one bit. It’s like somebody just flipped a switch and turned off all my get-up-and-go. I used to be the first one at the church for the spring rummage sale setup, dragging those big heavy folding tables out of the fellowship hall basement, and now... well, like I said, I just sit in that brown recliner and watch the dust motes float in the sunlight. But no, sir, never in the past. This is the very first time I've ever felt this way, and I certainly don't care for it.
=====================================
